Local Linear Convergence of the Alternating Direction Method of Multipliers for Semidefinite Programming under Strict Complementarity
Shucheng Kang, Xin Jiang, Heng Yang

Abstract
We investigate the local linear convergence properties of the Alternating Direction Method of Multipliers (ADMM) when applied to Semidefinite Programming (SDP). A longstanding belief suggests that ADMM is only capable of solving SDPs to moderate accuracy, primarily due to its sublinear worst-case complexity and empirical observations of slow convergence. We challenge this notion by introducing a new sufficient condition for local linear convergence: as long as the converged primal–dual optimal solutions satisfy strict complementarity, ADMM attains local linear convergence, independent of nondegeneracy conditions. Our proof is based on a direct local linearization of the ADMM operator and a refined error bound for the projection onto the positive semidefinite cone, improving previous bounds and revealing the anisotropic nature of projection residuals. Extensive numerical experiments confirm the significance of our theoretical results, demonstrating that ADMM achieves local linear convergence and computes high-accuracy solutions in a variety of SDP instances, including those where nondegeneracy fails. Furthermore, we identify cases where ADMM struggles, linking these difficulties with near violations of strict complementarity—a phenomenon that parallels recent findings in linear programming.

1.1
ADMM for SDP: Sublinear Rate and Moderate Accuracy?

A common perception for ADMM among practitioners is that it generally cannot solve the SDP (
1
) to high accuracy (
e.g.,
max KKT residual below
\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28720^{\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28720}
). Indeed, this perception arises from both theoretical challenges and empirical observations.

Theoretically, a well-accepted convergence rate for ADMM applied to solving SDPs is
{\cal\mathchar 29007}\left\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28721\delimiter 68408078\mathchar 28943\right\delimiter 84054785
, which matches the general convex optimization case
[
53
]
. Establishing linear convergence
{\cal\mathchar 29007}\delimiter 67273472\log\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28721\delimiter 68408078\mathchar 28943\delimiter 84054785\delimiter 84054785
is considered hard, since known sufficient conditions, such as strong convexity
[
46
]
, local polyhedrality
[
37
,
29
]
and certain growth conditions
[
17
,
29
,
15
,
41
,
68
,
69
]
, either fail or remain unclear for SDP. As pioneering works,
[
11
,
24
]
established local linear convergence of ADMM for solving SDPs when primal nondegeneracy (
8
) and dual nondegeneracy (
9
) both hold (regardless of strict complementarity (
10
)), by showing the metric subregularity (or calmness) of the KKT operator. However, two challenges remain: (a) two-side nondegeneracy conditions are hard to check numerically, and (b) important subclasses of SDP, such as those arising from the moment-SOS relaxations with finite convergence
[
34
]
, are known to be degenerate. It remains an open question in optimization theory whether alternative, and perhaps simple-to-verify, sufficient conditions can ensure the linear convergence of ADMM for solving SDPs.

Empirically, the slow convergence of ADMM and its variants when solving SDPs is widely reported
[
22
,
33
,
72
,
66
]
. For a typical SDP, the interior point method
[
3
]
(IPM) implemented in
MOSEK

[
5
]
can solve the problem to machine precision (if memory permits), while ADMM often struggles to achieve moderate accuracy (e.g., max KKT residual
\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28720^{\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28724}
). Consequently, ADMM and its variants are primarily used to warmstart downstream solvers
[
67
,
66
]
or as efficient methods to obtain coarse solutions
[
33
]
. In contrast, recently in the linear programming (LP) literature, first-order methods are observed to exhibit significantly improved local linear convergence rates after initially traversing a prolonged phase of slow convergence
[
42
]
. To the best of our knowledge, no comparable numerical evidence has been documented in the SDP literature.

Thus, the central question driving this paper is:

Can ADMM exhibit empirically observable linear convergence when solving SDPs? If so, can we establish numerically verifiable sufficient conditions to guarantee this behavior?

1.2
Contributions

We affirm this question both empirically and theoretically. We establish local linear convergence of ADMM for SDP under a mild strict complementarity (SC) assumption. Moreover, comprehensive numerical results are reported to demonstrate such a prevalent linear rate of convergence.

Theoretical contribution.

We establish a new sufficient condition that guarantees the local linear convergence of ADMM for solving SDPs.

Theorem 1

(Informal: Local Linear Convergence under Strict Complementarity)
.

If ADMM converges to an optimal solution
\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29016_{\mathchar 8511}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29049_{\mathchar 8511}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29011_{\mathchar 8511}\delimiter 84054785
of the SDP (
1
) that satisfies strict complementarity (
10
), then ADMM attains local linear convergence after finite iterations.

Our sufficient condition holds independently of nondegeneracy (ND). Verifying strict complementarity (
10
) requires only checking the numerical rank of
\mathchar 29016_{\mathchar 8511}
and
\mathchar 29011_{\mathchar 8511}
, a significantly more tractable procedure compared with verifying nondegeneracy, which involves examining the intersection of two subspaces.

At a high level, our proof framework is built upon a detailed local linearization analysis of the ADMM operator, incorporating several key contributions.

•

A refined error bound for the PSD cone projector.

We improve the classic linearization result of the PSD cone projector in
[
56
, Theorem 4.6]
by tightening the bound on the linearization residual from
{\cal\mathchar 29007}\delimiter 67273472\delimiter 69645069\mathchar 29000\delimiter 86422285^{\mathchar 28722}\delimiter 84054785
to
{\cal\mathchar 29007}\delimiter 67273472\delimiter 69645069\mathchar 29000_{\mathchar 29007}\delimiter 86422285\mathchar 8705\delimiter 69645069\mathchar 29000\delimiter 86422285\delimiter 84054785
, where
\mathchar 29000
represents a small perturbation and
\mathchar 29000_{\mathchar 29007}
denotes its off-block-diagonal part. This refinement is a cornerstone of our proof, revealing the anisotropic nature of the residual and offering potential applications beyond our setting.

•

Characterization of the local behavior of ADMM.

Our analysis relies on a local linearization of the fixed-point iteration (
9
). In particular, when near optimum, the iterate error
\mathchar 29018^{\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29035\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721\delimiter 84054785}\mathchar 8704\mathchar 29018_{\mathchar 8511}
can be written as a linear transformation of
\mathchar 29018^{\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29035\delimiter 84054785}\mathchar 8704\mathchar 29018_{\mathchar 8511}
plus a quadratic residual term. When both ND and SC hold, we prove that the linear transformation is contractive and that the linearization residual becomes negligible, directly leading to local linear convergence.

•

Handling cases without nondegeneracy.

When ND fails but SC holds, we first show that the several “partial” sequences,
e.g.,
the off-block-diagonal part of
\mathchar 29018^{\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29035\delimiter 84054785}\mathchar 8704\mathchar 29018_{\mathchar 8511}
, vanish at a local linear rate. To establish the convergence of the full sequence, we further extend the regularized backward error bound for spectrahedra
[
55
, Lemma 2.3]
to the (scaled) KKT system of SDP. This helps build a local “conditional sharpness” property, analogous to sharpness in LP
[
4
]
but with an additional term related to the minimal faces of the PSD cone. This “conditional sharpness”, together with the convergence of “partial” sequences, finally yields the R-linear rate of convergence for the ADMM iterates.

Notably, our proof framework differs from that of
[
24
]
in that it does not directly rely on metric subregularity of the KKT operator, which is generally intractable to verify numerically. At the end of the paper, we also briefly discuss the relationship between our framework and several local error bounds in the ADMM literature. In particular, in the ADMM-for-SDP setting, we show that strict complementarity at the limiting KKT point implies a weak local error bound for the DRS operator.

Empirical contribution.

In parallel with the theoretical findings, we present extensive numerical evidence demonstrating the prevalent local linear convergence of ADMM when solving SDPs. To systematically analyze this phenomenon, we categorize SDP problems into four families based on whether the nondegeneracy (ND) and strict complementarity (SC) conditions hold or fail. A representative subset of our numerical results is shown in
Figure

1
, where ADMM consistently enters a linear convergence regime across all four cases. The full set of numerical experiments is detailed in
Section

8
, covering a broad range of SDP instances. Our test suite includes standard benchmark datasets
[
50
,
44
]
as well as newly generated SDP problems in real-world applications
[
25
,
65
]
. These instances span classical MAXCUT-style SDPs and more challenging problems from the moment-SOS relaxations with finite convergence
[
34
,
59
]
. All the SDP problems are available at

https://github.com/ComputationalRobotics/admmsdp-linearconv
.

(a) ND holds and SC holds

(b) ND holds and SC fails

(c) ND fails and SC holds

(d) ND fails and SC fails

Figure 1:
Four representative SDP instances. (a) A toy structure-from-motion problem from
[
25
]
; (b) A toy example from
[
62
, pp. 44]
; (c) A Quasar problem from
[
64
]
with random initialization; (d) Second-order relaxation for a random BQP problem
[
66
]
with all-zeros initialization. Here,
\mathchar 29042_{\mathrm{\mathchar 29037\mathchar 29025\mathchar 29048}}
denotes the maximum KKT residual. In all the cases, ADMM with fixed
\mathchar 28955
parameter eventually exhibits local linear convergence.

In addition, we document “failure” cases of ADMM in which the maximum KKT residual remains above
\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28720^{\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28720}
even after reaching the iteration limit (
\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28720^{\mathchar 28726}
iterations) or the time limit (100 hours). These cases exhibit a common feature: the minimum positive eigenvalue of the converged
\mathchar 29016_{\mathchar 8511}
or
\mathchar 29011_{\mathchar 8511}
is near zero. This behavior closely resembles difficult cases in LP
[
42
]
and can be partly explained via our proof framework.

Open questions: rank identification and beyond.

In first-order methods for LP
[
42
]
, local linear convergence is achieved alongside with basis identification. An analogous result in ADMM for SDP (with SC) would be
rank identification
;
i.e.,
after a finite number of iterations, ADMM identifies the solution rank, and all subsequent iterates maintain at the same rank. Though this result could be readily drawn from the partial smoothness theory
[
63
,
35
,
20
]
, we provide a more direct proof that only leverages the algorithmic properties of ADMM. However, unlike the LP case, it remains unclear that whether rank identification and linear convergence occur
simultaneously
in ADMM for SDP. In this work, we provide a partial answer through a numerical example and leave a full investigation for future work.

Relating our discussion back to
Figure

1
, this work establishes (R-)linear convergence guarantees of ADMM for SDP, which covers cases (a)–(c) in
Figure

1
. (In comparison,
[
24
]
explains cases (a) and (b).) However, whether ADMM provably attains local linear convergence in case (d), where both ND and SC fail, remains an open question, a gap between theory and practice that warrants further investigation.

1.4
Outline

After a brief review of related work in
Section

2
, we introduce our refined error bound in
Section

3
, a fundamental result that underpins our proof framework and holds independent interest. We then examine the local linearization of ADMM in
Section

4
and establish its local linear convergence both with and without nondegeneracy in
Section

5
and
Section

6
, respectively. Due to its mathematical complexity, the full proof of our refined error bound is deferred to
Section

7
. In
Section

8
, we conduct extensive numerical experiments to support our theoretical findings.
Section

9
briefly discusses the rank identification phenomenon as well as its relationship with local linear convergence.
Section

10
briefly discusses the relationship between strict complementarity and metric subregularity, local error bounds, and hidden polyhedrality.
Finally,
Section

11
includes concluding remarks.

2
Related Work

With the rapid development of data science and all fields in engineering, SDP problems are growing in scale. Efficient and scalable algorithms have been developed, analyzed and implemented for solving large-scale SDPs.

Augmented Lagrangian method (ALM).

Originally introduced to enhance the performance of penalty methods
[
51
]
, ALM has shown promise in tackling large-scale SDPs
[
67
]
. Under mild conditions (strong duality and the existence of a strictly complementary solution pair), linear convergence of ALM
[
71
,
14
,
39
]
is established by leveraging its connection to proximal point methods
[
52
]
(PPM) and quadratic growth properties
[
55
,
19
]
.

Burer–Monteiro (BM) factorization method.

The BM method
[
10
]
replaces the conic constraint by
\mathchar 29016\mathchar 12349\mathchar 29010\mathchar 29010^{\mathsf{\mathchar 29012}}
, reducing the problem to a lower-dimensional nonlinear program. Under specific rank and regularity conditions, it recovers the global optimum of the original SDP
[
9
,
57
]
. Owing to its efficiency, the BM method has achieved significant empirical success in real-world problems with low-rank solutions
[
25
]
. It can also be combined with ALM
[
59
]
or ADMM
[
26
]
.

Spectral bundle method (SBM).

First proposed in
[
27
]
, SBM has gained attention for its low per-iteration cost. It enjoys sublinear convergence under mild assumptions
[
18
]
. Furthermore, if a strictly complementary solution pair exists and the surrogate function captures the correct rank of the optimal solution, SBM achieves local linear convergence
[
18
,
38
]
. Similar to ALM, its linear convergence guarantees rely on quadratic growth. Recent work also incorporates SBM into ALM
[
40
]
.

First-order proximal methods and (local) linear convergence of ADMM.

As a broad class of first-order methods derived from the monotone operator theory
[
53
]
, primal–dual proximal methods are widely used for SDP. In addition to ADMM
[
61
]
, symmetric Gauss-Seidel (sGS)-ADMM
[
13
]
has gained traction for solving general SDPs to medium accuracy, and its connection to proximal ALM is discussed in
[
12
]
. Other proximal methods, such as the primal–dual hybrid gradient (PDHG) method
[
31
]
, have also been explored. On the theoretical side, linear convergence of ADMM was first established for structured problems such as linear and quadratic programs
[
8
]
, and later extended to strongly convex settings under additional smoothness and rank conditions
[
17
,
46
]
. Subsequent local and variational-analytic analyses further identified sufficient conditions based on geometric regularity, error bounds, calmness, and metric-subregularity
[
37
,
15
,
29
,
41
,
68
,
24
]
. Nonetheless, establishing local linear convergence remains much harder for SDP than for ALM or SBM. In particular, the known sufficient conditions for linear convergence of ADMM,
e.g.,
strong convexity
[
46
]
, local polyhedrality
[
37
]
and other growth conditions
[
17
,
29
,
15
,
41
,
68
,
69
]
, either fail or remain unclear for SDP. See
Section

10
for more discussion.

3
A Refined Error Bound for PSD Cone Projection

We see from (
4
) that the only nonlinear operation in ADMM is the projection onto the PSD cone. So, to better understand the convergence of ADMM for SDP, we need to study the local behavior of the PSD cone projector
\mathchar 28677_{\mathbb{\mathchar 29011}^{\mathchar 29038}_{\mathchar 8235}}
. A classic result on the perturbation theory of
\mathchar 28677_{\mathbb{\mathchar 29011}^{\mathchar 29038}_{\mathchar 8235}}
is
[
56
, Theorem 4.6]
, which is restated below for self-containment.

[

, Theorem 4.6]
)
.

Moreover, for any sufficiently small perturbation
\mathchar 29000\mathchar 12850\mathbb{\mathchar 29011}^{\mathchar 29038}
, it holds that

\delimiter 69645069\mathchar 28677_{\mathbb{\mathchar 29011}^{\mathchar 29038}_{\mathchar 8235}}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29018\mathchar 8235\mathchar 29000\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28677_{\mathbb{\mathchar 29011}^{\mathchar 29038}_{\mathchar 8235}}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29018\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 8704\mathchar 29009\big\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28682\mathchar 8718\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29009^{\mathsf{\mathchar 29012}}\mathchar 29000\mathchar 29009\delimiter 84054785\big\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 29009^{\mathsf{\mathchar 29012}}\delimiter 86422285_{\mathchar 28722}\mathchar 12349{\cal\mathchar 29007}\delimiter 67273472\delimiter 69645069\mathchar 29000\delimiter 86422285_{\mathchar 28722}^{\mathchar 28722}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 314

However, the following simple example illustrates that the above result may not be tight and motivates our refined error bound. To see this, assume for brevity that
\mathchar 29009\mathchar 12349\mathchar 29001
in
Lemma

1
and partition the perturbation
\mathchar 29000\mathchar 12850\mathbb{\mathchar 29011}^{\mathchar 29038}

Remark 3
.

In
[
14
, Proposition 3.4]
, the authors establish another perturbation property for the PSD cone projector. Their result has two key distinctions from
Theorem

2
: (1) their results cover cases where
\mathchar 29018
is singular, while ours only focuses on the nonsingular case; (2) under the nonsingularity assumption, our results can directly lead to theirs;
i.e.,

Theorem

2
is stronger than
[
14
, Proposition 3.4]
. See
Appendix

A
for detailed discussion.

4
Local Linearization of ADMM

With a better understanding of the PSD cone projection, we now study the local behavior of ADMM. Our analysis is different from the standard approaches for ADMM and starts by locally linearizing the iteration (
4
). In particular, we show that when near optimum, the residual
\mathchar 29000^{\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29035\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721\delimiter 84054785}\mathchar 12346\mathchar 12349\mathchar 29018^{\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29035\delimiter 84054785}\mathchar 8704\mathchar 29018_{\mathchar 8511}
is almost a linear transformation of the previous residual
\mathchar 29000^{\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29035\delimiter 84054785}
, plus a correction term in the order of
{\cal\mathchar 29007}\delimiter 67273472\delimiter 69645069\mathchar 29000_{\mathchar 29007}\delimiter 86422285_{\mathsf{\mathchar 28998}}\delimiter 69645069\mathchar 29000\delimiter 86422285_{\mathsf{\mathchar 28998}}\delimiter 84054785
.

The rest of the section is devoted to study this linearization and is organized as follows.
Section

4.1
lists all the assumptions made throughout the paper,
Section

4.2
presents the local linearization of ADMM, and
Section

4.3
describes the properties of such a linearization.

4.1
Assumptions

We make the following assumption on the pair of primal–dual SDPs (
1
).

Assumption 1
.

(a)

The linear operator
{\cal\mathchar 28993}\mathchar 12346\mathbb{\mathchar 29011}^{\mathchar 29038}\mathchar 12833{{\mathbb{\mathchar 29010}}^{\mathchar 29037}}
is surjective.

(b)

The pair of primal–dual SDPs (
1
) has a nonempty set of KKT points.

From convex duality theory, any pair of primal–dual solutions
\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29016_{\mathchar 8511}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29049_{\mathchar 8511}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29011_{\mathchar 8511}\delimiter 84054785
of (
1
) satisfies complementary slackness;
i.e.,

\mathchar 29016_{\mathchar 8511}
and
\mathchar 29011_{\mathchar 8511}
admit the decompositions in (
6
).
Moreover,
\left\delimiter 0\mathchar 29016_{\mathchar 8511}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29011_{\mathchar 8511}\right\delimiter 0
and
\mathrm{\mathchar 29042\mathchar 29025\mathchar 29038\mathchar 29035}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29016_{\mathchar 8511}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 8235\mathrm{\mathchar 29042\mathchar 29025\mathchar 29038\mathchar 29035}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29011_{\mathchar 8511}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12820\mathchar 29038
. When the above inequality holds with equality, the solution pair
\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29016_{\mathchar 8511}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29049_{\mathchar 8511}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29011_{\mathchar 8511}\delimiter 84054785
is called strictly complementary.

It is known that under
Assumption

1
, three-step ADMM (
2
) converges to a KKT point
\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29016_{\mathchar 8511}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29049_{\mathchar 8511}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29011_{\mathchar 8511}\delimiter 84054785
, or equivalently, one-step ADMM (
4
) converges to the point
\mathchar 29018_{\mathchar 8511}\mathchar 12346\mathchar 12349\mathchar 29016_{\mathchar 8511}\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28955\mathchar 29018_{\mathchar 8511}
. Our analysis assumes additionally that the convergent point of ADMM is strictly complementary.

Assumption 2
.

Three-step ADMM (
2
) converges to a KKT point
\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29016_{\mathchar 8511}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29049_{\mathchar 8511}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29011_{\mathchar 8511}\delimiter 84054785
satisfying strict complementarity;
i.e.,
,
\mathrm{\mathchar 29042\mathchar 29025\mathchar 29038\mathchar 29035}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29016_{\mathchar 8511}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 8235\mathrm{\mathchar 29042\mathchar 29025\mathchar 29038\mathchar 29035}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29011_{\mathchar 8511}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349\mathchar 29038
.

Assumption

2
is equivalent to the condition that the convergent point
\mathchar 29018_{\mathchar 8511}
of one-step ADMM (
4
) is nonsingular.
Assumption

2
is a mild assumption in the sense that it holds for generic SDPs
[
2
]
(
i.e.,
the set of SDP data
\delimiter 67273472{\cal\mathchar 28993}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29026\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28995\delimiter 84054785
that fails strict complementarity is of Lebesque measure zero)
. Numerical experiments in
Section

8
further demonstrate that even for degenerate SDPs with multiple solutions, one-step ADMM often converges to a nonsingular
\mathchar 29018_{\mathchar 8511}
(if one exists) when initialized with a random (standard Gaussian) guess
\mathchar 29018^{\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28720\delimiter 84054785}
.

For ease of presentation, we assume without loss of generality that the convergent points

and

are diagonal,

Remark 4
.

One may notice that Assumption

e.g.,
metric subregularity
[
41
]
and two-sided nondegeneracy
[
24
]
), is
post hoc
: it can generally be verified only after the algorithm has converged. This is largely unavoidable, because local linear convergence is inherently a statement about the geometry in a neighborhood of the limit point. For example, there exist simple SDP instances
[
14
, Example 1]
in which metric subregularity of the KKT operator fails at a particular KKT point, while holding at the remaining points in the optimal set.
Compared with other regularity conditions, Assumption
2
has two main advantages:

•

It can be checked efficiently in practice. Given an ADMM iterate
\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29016^{\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29035\delimiter 84054785}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29049^{\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29035\delimiter 84054785}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29011^{\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29035\delimiter 84054785}\delimiter 84054785
near a KKT point, one needs only
{\cal\mathchar 29007}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29038^{\mathchar 28723}\delimiter 84054785
time to determine whether strict complementarity holds, via a numerical full-rankness check on
\mathchar 29016^{\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29035\delimiter 84054785}\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28955\mathchar 29011^{\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29035\delimiter 84054785}
. By contrast, checking two-sided nondegeneracy requires
{\cal\mathchar 29007}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29038^{\mathchar 28726}\delimiter 84054785
time because one must form a basis for
{\cal\mathchar 29012}_{\mathchar 29016^{\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29035\delimiter 84054785}}
and
{\cal\mathchar 29012}_{\mathchar 29011^{\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29035\delimiter 84054785}}
. General metric subregularity, on the other hand, is typically not directly verifiable in computation.

•

In a wide range of real-world SDPs, two-sided nondegeneracy is known to fail
[
64
,
33
]
. By contrast, strictly complementary solutions still exist for a substantial subset of such degenerate SDPs
[
18
]
.

In Section
10
, we will also see connections between strict complementarity and several forms of metric subregularity/local error bounds.

5
Local Linear Convergence with Nondegeneracy

In this section, we establish local linear convergence of ADMM when primal and dual nondegeneracy holds at optimum. In this case, the pair of SDPs (
1
) has a unique KKT point and
Assumption

2
is equivalent to merely existence of a strictly complementary solution, which is a common regularity condition for SDP in the literature.

We start with the simple characteristic of
\mathrm{\mathchar 28998\mathchar 29033\mathchar 29048}\delimiter 67273472{\cal\mathchar 29005}\delimiter 84054785
when nondegeneracy holds.

Remark 5
.

Our proof framework can also cover the case where ND holds and SC fails. To stay consistent with our SC assumption, the detailed proof of this case is deferred to
Appendix

B
. So, combining
Theorem

3
and the results in
Appendix

B
, we establish local linear convergence of ADMM for SDP under only the nondegeneracy conditions. Though this conclusion can be drawn from
[
24
]
, our proof techniques are completely different from theirs and do not involve the metric subregularity of the KKT operator. Moreover, numerical evidence is provided in
Section

8
to support the theoretical findings in
Appendix

B
.

6
Local R-linear Convergence without Nondegeneracy

Without two-side nondegeneracy,
\mathrm{\mathchar 28998\mathchar 29033\mathchar 29048}\delimiter 67273472{\cal\mathchar 29005}\delimiter 84054785
is not
\left\{\mathchar 28720\right\}
and the proof technique in

does not apply anymore. Another nice property of

in

. Specifically, this property motivates us to study the “projected sequence”:

\displaystyle\delimiter 67273472\mathrm{\mathchar 29001\mathchar 29028}\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28677_{\mathrm{\mathchar 28998\mathchar 29033\mathchar 29048}\delimiter 67273472{\cal\mathchar 29005}\delimiter 84054785}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 29000^{\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29035\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721\delimiter 84054785}

So, what is missing in the more general, possibly degenerate case? It turns out that an error bound for
\mathchar 28677_{\mathbb{\mathchar 29011}^{\mathchar 29038}_{\mathchar 8235}}
alone is insufficient; a growth condition is needed that accounts for both the PSD cone and an affine set. More specifically, consider the spectrahedron
{\cal\mathchar 29014}\mathchar 8796\mathbb{\mathchar 29011}^{\mathchar 29038}_{\mathchar 8235}
, where
{\cal\mathchar 29014}
is an affine space in
\mathbb{\mathchar 29011}^{\mathchar 29038}
. Following the convention in
[
55
]
, we call
\mathrm{\mathchar 29028\mathchar 29033\mathchar 29043\mathchar 29044}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29016\mathchar 24891{\cal\mathchar 29014}\mathchar 8796\mathbb{\mathchar 29011}^{\mathchar 29038}_{\mathchar 8235}\delimiter 84054785
the
forward error
and
\mathrm{\mathchar 29028\mathchar 29033\mathchar 29043\mathchar 29044}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29016\mathchar 24891{\cal\mathchar 29014}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 8235\delimiter 67482370\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28949_{\min}\left\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29016\right\delimiter 84054785\delimiter 84267779_{\mathchar 8235}
the
backward error
. In the polyhedral case
(
i.e.,

\mathbb{\mathchar 29011}^{\mathchar 29038}_{\mathchar 8235}
reduces to the nonnegative orthant), the backward error and the forward error are in the same order
[
28
]
, which leads to the
sharpness
condition and linear convergence of first-order methods in linear programming
[
4
]
. In the spectrahedron case, however, it is shown in
[
55
]
that

\text{forward error}\mathchar 12349{\cal\mathchar 29007}\big\delimiter 67273472\delimiter 67273472\text{backward error}\delimiter 84054785^{\mathchar 28721\delimiter 68408078\mathchar 28722}\big\delimiter 84054785

under mild conditions.
So SDPs are not sharp in general and linear convergence does not follow in a straightforward manner.

Fortunately, by exploiting the geometry of the PSD cone, it is shown in
[
55
, Lemma 2.3]
that the forward error is in the same order as the backward error with respect to the regularized system

{\cal\mathchar 29014}\mathchar 8796\mathrm{\mathchar 29037\mathchar 29033\mathchar 29038\mathchar 29030\mathchar 29025\mathchar 29027\mathchar 29029}\left\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29016_{\mathchar 8511}\mathchar 24891\mathbb{\mathchar 29011}_{\mathchar 8235}^{\mathchar 29038}\right\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 24891\quad\text{where}\ \mathrm{\mathchar 29037\mathchar 29033\mathchar 29038\mathchar 29030\mathchar 29025\mathchar 29027\mathchar 29029}\left\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29016_{\mathchar 8511}\mathchar 24891\mathbb{\mathchar 29011}_{\mathchar 8235}^{\mathchar 29038}\right\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12346\mathchar 12349\left\{\begin{\mathchar29026\mathchar29037\mathchar29025\mathchar29044\mathchar29042\mathchar29033\mathchar29048}\mathchar 28672&\mathchar 28720\\
\mathchar 28720&\mathchar 28720\end{\mathchar29026\mathchar29037\mathchar29025\mathchar29044\mathchar29042\mathchar29033\mathchar29048}\Bigm\mathchar 28672\mathchar 12850\mathbb{\mathchar 29011}_{\mathchar 8235}^{\mathchar 29042}\right\}\mathchar 12349\mathbb{\mathchar 29011}_{\mathchar 8235}^{\mathchar 29038}\mathchar 8796{\cal\mathchar 29012}_{\mathchar 29011_{\mathchar 8511}}^{\mathchar 12863}\mathchar 314

(The simple characteristic of the minimal face needs the assumption, made without loss of generality, that

is diagonal.) Extending the conclusion in

Remark 7
.

We note that our current analysis framework is intentionally restricted to a fixed penalty parameter
\mathchar 28955
, since the linearization is built around a stationary fixed-point map. If
\mathchar 28955
varies with the iteration index, then the underlying operator becomes time-varying, and the present contraction and
\mathrm{\mathchar 28998\mathchar 29033\mathchar 29048}\delimiter 67273472{\cal\mathchar 29005}\delimiter 84054785
arguments no longer apply directly. Extending our framework to the adaptive-
\mathchar 28955
setting would therefore be an interesting direction for future work.

7
Proof of the Refined Error Bound

In this section, we detail the proof of
Theorem

2
, which builds an error bound for the PSD cone projection:

\delimiter 69645069\mathchar 28677_{\mathbb{\mathchar 29011}^{\mathchar 29038}_{\mathchar 8235}}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29018\mathchar 8235\mathchar 29000\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28677_{\mathbb{\mathchar 29011}^{\mathchar 29038}_{\mathchar 8235}}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29018\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28682\mathchar 8718\mathchar 29000\delimiter 86422285_{\mathchar 28722}\mathchar 12820\mathchar 28939_{\mathrm{\mathchar 28997\mathchar 28994}}\mathchar 8705\delimiter 69645069\mathchar 29000_{\mathchar 29007}\delimiter 86422285_{\mathchar 28722}\mathchar 8705\delimiter 69645069\mathchar 29000\delimiter 86422285_{\mathchar 28722}\mathchar 24891

where we have assumed without loss of generality that
\mathchar 29018
is diagonal. A traditional way to compute the orthogonal projection onto the PSD cone is via eigenvalue decomposition. Though conceptually simple, this method destroys the block structure of
\mathchar 29018\mathchar 8235\mathchar 29000
(as
\mathchar 29000
is not diagonal). So, to prove
Theorem

2
, we advocate for a seemingly much more complex procedure inspired by iterative methods for eigenvalue decomposition (see,
e.g.,

[
49
]
). We detail the iterative algorithm in
Algorithm
1
and briefly discuss the high-level intuition here.

Figure 2:

Visualization of the elimination procedure (Algorithm
1
).

A roadmap to the proof of
Theorem

2
.
A standard way to project onto the PSD cone is via eigenvalue decomposition. Although conceptually simple, this destroys the block structure of
\mathchar 29018\mathchar 8235\mathchar 29000
because
\mathchar 29000
is not diagonal. To prove
Theorem

2
, we instead use a more structured procedure inspired by iterative methods for eigenvalue decomposition; see,
e.g.,

[
49
]
. As detailed in Algorithm
1
, in the eigenbasis of
\mathchar 29018
, the perturbation
\mathchar 29000
is nearly block diagonal, and its off-block-diagonal part is the only term coupling the positive and negative spectral subspaces. Each Sylvester equation then produces a small skew-symmetric rotation that cancels this coupling to first order, leaving only higher-order terms. Iterating this step yields a rapidly convergent block diagonalization; see
Figure

2
for an illustration. Overall, this construction locally mimics PSD cone projection while isolating the first- and second-order terms needed in the proof.

\mathchar 29018\mathchar 8235\mathchar 29000\mathchar 12349\mathrm{\mathchar 29028\mathchar 29033\mathchar 29025\mathchar 29031}\left\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28949_{\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 24891\ldots\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28949_{\mathchar 29042}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28949_{\mathchar 29042\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 24891\ldots\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28949_{\mathchar 29038}\right\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 8235\begin{\mathchar29026\mathchar29037\mathchar29025\mathchar29044\mathchar29042\mathchar29033\mathchar29048}\mathchar 29000_{\mathchar 29016}&\mathchar 29000_{\mathchar 29007}^{\mathsf{\mathchar 29012}}\\
\mathchar 29000_{\mathchar 29007}&\mathchar 29000_{\mathchar 29011}\end{\mathchar29026\mathchar29037\mathchar29025\mathchar29044\mathchar29042\mathchar29033\mathchar29048}\mathchar 12349\mathchar 12346\begin{\mathchar29026\mathchar29037\mathchar29025\mathchar29044\mathchar29042\mathchar29033\mathchar29048}\mathchar 29018_{\mathchar 29016\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28720}&\mathchar 29018_{\mathchar 29007\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28720}^{\mathsf{\mathchar 29012}}\\
\mathchar 29018_{\mathchar 29007\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28720}&\mathchar 29018_{\mathchar 29011\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28720}\end{\mathchar29026\mathchar29037\mathchar29025\mathchar29044\mathchar29042\mathchar29033\mathchar29048}\mathchar 314

(34)

When
\delimiter 69645069\mathchar 29000\delimiter 86422285_{\mathchar 28722}
is sufficiently small, we have
\mathchar 29018_{\mathchar 29016\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28720}\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28675_{\mathchar 29016}\mathchar 8235\mathchar 29000_{\mathchar 29016}\mathchar 12850\mathbb{\mathchar 29011}_{\mathchar 8235\mathchar 8235}^{\mathchar 29042}
and
\mathchar 29018_{\mathchar 29011\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28720}\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28675_{\mathchar 29011}\mathchar 8235\mathchar 29000_{\mathchar 29011}\mathchar 12850\mathbb{\mathchar 29011}_{\mathchar 8704\mathchar 8704}^{\mathchar 29038\mathchar 8704\mathchar 29042}

(35)

(So roughly speaking,

\mathchar 28677_{\mathbb{\mathchar 29011}^{\mathchar 29038}_{\mathchar 8235}}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29018\mathchar 8235\mathchar 29000\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28677_{\mathbb{\mathchar 29011}^{\mathchar 29038}_{\mathchar 8235}}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29018\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28682\mathchar 8718\mathchar 29000\mathchar 12349\mathchar 29009_{\mathchar 561}\begin{\mathchar29026\mathchar29037\mathchar29025\mathchar29044\mathchar29042\mathchar29033\mathchar29048}\mathchar 29018_{\mathchar 29016\mathchar 24891\mathchar 561}&\mathchar 28720\\

which further leads to the conclusion in

.

Algorithm 1

An iterative elimination procedure for PSD cone projection

\mathchar 29018\mathchar 12850\mathbb{\mathchar 29011}^{\mathchar 29038}
with
\mathchar 29042

\mathchar 29000\mathchar 12850\mathbb{\mathchar 29011}^{\mathchar 29038}

\mathchar 29018_{\mathchar 28720}\mathchar 12346\mathchar 12349\begin{\mathchar29026\mathchar29037\mathchar29025\mathchar29044\mathchar29042\mathchar29033\mathchar29048}\mathchar 29018_{\mathchar 29016\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28720}&\mathchar 29018_{\mathchar 29007\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28720}^{\mathsf{\mathchar 29012}}\\

and
\mathchar 29009_{\mathchar 28720}\mathchar 12832\mathchar 29001_{\mathchar 29038}
.

2

for

\mathchar 352\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28720
to
\mathchar 561

do

8
Numerical Experiments

In this section, numerical evidence is reported to support our theoretical findings. In particular, numerical experiments are conducted to demonstrate the following.

1.

Local (R-)linear convergence is observed, regardless of the (non)degeneracy of the SDP.

2.

The established (R-)linear rate of convergence (
e.g.,
in
Theorem

3
and
Lemma

5
) is numerically tight.

3.

When SC is close to failure, ADMM for SDP may be extremely slow and no clear linear convergence can be observed within the stated computational budget.

Experiments are performed on a high-performance workstation equipped with a 2.7 GHz AMD 64-Core sWRX8 Processor and 1 TB of RAM. For the standard SDP (
1
), we denote primal infeasibility
\mathchar 29042_{\mathrm{\mathchar 29040}}
, dual infeasibility
\mathchar 29042_{\mathrm{\mathchar 29028}}
, and relative gap
\mathchar 29042_{\mathrm{\mathchar 29031\mathchar 29025\mathchar 29040}}
as:

Additional numerical results can be found in
Appendix

E
.

One may also notice two interesting empirical phenomena across a broad class of degenerate SDPs (
e.g.,
BQP and Quasar):

•

ADMM often converges to a strictly complementary primal–dual solution pair when initialized from a random Gaussian point. At present, this is a
purely empirical
observation, and we view this apparent solution-selection tendency as an interesting direction for future study.

•

Even when ADMM converges to a primal–dual solution pair that is not strictly complementary, linear convergence is still often observed. This suggests that
Theorem

4
provides only a
sufficient
condition for local linear convergence, rather than a necessary one. Further relaxing the strict complementarity assumption would therefore be an intriguing direction for future work.

8.3
“Failure”
Cases

In
Figure

13
, we report some SDP instances for which ADMM fails to achieve
\mathchar 29042_{\mathrm{\mathchar 29037\mathchar 29025\mathchar 29048}}\mathchar 12820\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28720^{\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28720}
within the stated budget and no clear linear convergence is observed. A common feature in these instances is that the values
\mathchar 28949_{\min}\left\delimiter 67273472\left\delimiter 69640972\mathchar 29018_{\mathchar 8511}\right\delimiter 69640972\right\delimiter 84054785
tend to be small (
e.g.,

\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28720^{\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28724}\mathchar 12824\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28720^{\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28729}
), yet not exactly zero (compared to QS and BQP cases where
\mathchar 28949_{\min}\left\delimiter 67273472\left\delimiter 69640972\mathchar 29018_{\mathchar 8511}\right\delimiter 69640972\right\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12604\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28720^{\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28721\mathchar 28724}
).

There are two possible explanations for the observed slow convergence: (i) ADMM has already entered the linear-convergence regime, but the rate is close to
\mathchar 28721
, so the method essentially stalls; or (ii) the local linear-convergence regime of ADMM, if it exists, has not yet emerged. We conjecture that much of the observed slow convergence falls into case (ii). Indeed,
Theorem

2
shows that the refined error bound holds only for sufficiently small perturbations satisfying
\delimiter 69645069\mathchar 29000\delimiter 69645069_{\mathchar 28722}\mathchar 12820\mathchar 28995_{\mathrm{\mathchar 28997\mathchar 28994}}
, where
\mathchar 28995_{\mathrm{\mathchar 28997\mathchar 28994}}
is proportional to
\min\{\mathchar 28949_{\mathchar 29042}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28949_{\mathchar 29042\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721}\}
; see (
46
). Hence, when
\mathchar 28949_{\min}\left\delimiter 67273472\left\delimiter 69640972\mathchar 29018_{\mathchar 8511}\right\delimiter 69640972\right\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349\min\{\mathchar 28949_{\mathchar 29042}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28949_{\mathchar 29042\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721}\}
is small, the admissible perturbation radius
\mathchar 28995_{\mathrm{\mathchar 28997\mathchar 28994}}
is also small, so any eventual linear convergence of ADMM may appear only at a very late stage. This interpretation is also consistent with recent observations for first-order methods for LP
[
42
]
. At present, however, we do not have a rigorous theoretical proof of this conjecture.

cnhil10

neosfbr25

Cases from datasets

,

. For these SDPs, ADMM fails to achieve

within the stated budget, and no clear linear convergence is observed.

9
Discussion: Rank Identification and Linear Convergence

First-order methods (
e.g.,
PDHG) for LP (a special case of SDP) are known to have an intriguing two-stage phenomenon
[
42
]
. The first stage identifies the basis and finishes in a finite number of iterations, with a sublinear rate. Then, the second stage of the algorithm converges linearly, with a rate related to the local sharpness constant. In view of the equivalence between ADMM and PDHG
[
47
]
, as well as the similarity between the numerical results in
Section

8
and those in
[
42
]
, it is natural to ask whether ADMM for SDP has a similar two-stage performance and whether it could identify the solution
rank
(
c.f.
, basis in LP) within a finite number of iterations. This section aims to provide a partial answer to the above questions, both theoretically and empirically.

Finite-time rank identification.

In the context of ADMM for SDP, the fact that rank identification occurs within a finite number of iterations is readily guaranteed by the well-known partial smoothness theory
[
63
,
35
,
20
]
. More precisely, rank identification means that ADMM identifies the rank of the solution it converges to and all the subsequent ADMM iterates have the same rank. Here, we provide a more direct proof in the context of SDP, without invoking the more general partial smoothness theory.

Proposition 3
.

Suppose that
Assumptions

1
and
2
hold and that ADMM (
2
) converges to
\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29016_{\mathchar 8511}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29049_{\mathchar 8511}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29011_{\mathchar 8511}\delimiter 84054785
. Then, there exists
\bar{\mathchar 29035}_{\mathrm{\mathchar 29001\mathchar 28996}}\mathchar 12850\mathbb{\mathchar 29006}
such that for any integer
\mathchar 29035\mathchar 12821\bar{\mathchar 29035}_{\mathrm{\mathchar 29001\mathchar 28996}}
, it holds that

\mathrm{\mathchar 29042\mathchar 29025\mathchar 29038\mathchar 29035}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29016^{\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29035\delimiter 84054785}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349\mathrm{\mathchar 29042\mathchar 29025\mathchar 29038\mathchar 29035}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29016_{\mathchar 8511}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 24891\qquad\mathrm{\mathchar 29042\mathchar 29025\mathchar 29038\mathchar 29035}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29011^{\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29035\delimiter 84054785}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349\mathrm{\mathchar 29042\mathchar 29025\mathchar 29038\mathchar 29035}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29011_{\mathchar 8511}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 314

Proof.

First, we show that

\mathrm{\mathchar 29042\mathchar 29025\mathchar 29038\mathchar 29035}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28677_{\mathbb{\mathchar 29011}^{\mathchar 29038}_{\mathchar 8235}}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29018_{\mathchar 8511}\mathchar 8235\mathchar 29000^{\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29035\delimiter 84054785}\delimiter 84054785\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349\mathrm{\mathchar 29042\mathchar 29025\mathchar 29038\mathchar 29035}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28677_{\mathbb{\mathchar 29011}^{\mathchar 29038}_{\mathchar 8235}}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29018_{\mathchar 8511}\delimiter 84054785\delimiter 84054785\quad\text{if}\ \delimiter 69645069\mathchar 29000^{\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29035\delimiter 84054785}\delimiter 86422285_{\mathchar 28722}\mathchar 12604\min\{\mathchar 28949_{\mathchar 29042}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28949_{\mathchar 29042\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721}\}\mathchar 314

To see this, denote by
\mathchar 28941_{\mathchar 29042}
and
\mathchar 28941_{\mathchar 29042\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721}
the
\mathchar 29042
th and
\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29042\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721\delimiter 84054785
st largest eigenvalue of
\mathchar 29018_{\mathchar 8511}\mathchar 8235\mathchar 29000^{\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29035\delimiter 84054785}
, respectively. Then, by Weyl’s inequality, we have

\displaystyle\mathchar 28941_{\mathchar 29042}

\displaystyle\mathchar 12821\mathchar 28949_{\mathchar 29042}\mathchar 8704\delimiter 69645069\mathchar 29000^{\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29035\delimiter 84054785}\delimiter 86422285_{\mathchar 28722}\mathchar 12606\mathchar 28949_{\mathchar 29042}\mathchar 8704\min\{\mathchar 28949_{\mathchar 29042}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28949_{\mathchar 29042\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721}\}\mathchar 12821\mathchar 28720\mathchar 24891

\displaystyle\mathchar 28941_{\mathchar 29042\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721}

\displaystyle\mathchar 12820\mathchar 28949_{\mathchar 29042\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 8235\delimiter 69645069\mathchar 29000^{\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29035\delimiter 84054785}\delimiter 86422285_{\mathchar 28722}\mathchar 12604\mathchar 28949_{\mathchar 29042\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 8235\min\{\mathchar 28949_{\mathchar 29042}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28949_{\mathchar 29042\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721}\}\mathchar 12820\mathchar 28720\mathchar 24891

where recall
\mathchar 28949_{\mathchar 29042}
and
\mathchar 28949_{\mathchar 29042\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721}
are the
\mathchar 29042
th and
\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29042\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721\delimiter 84054785
st largest eigenvalue of
\mathchar 29018_{\mathchar 8511}
, respectively. Thus, we have
\mathchar 28941_{\mathchar 29042}\mathchar 12606\mathchar 28720\mathchar 12606\mathchar 28941_{\mathchar 29042\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721}
and

\mathrm{\mathchar 29042\mathchar 29025\mathchar 29038\mathchar 29035}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29016^{\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29035\delimiter 84054785}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349\mathrm{\mathchar 29042\mathchar 29025\mathchar 29038\mathchar 29035}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28677_{\mathbb{\mathchar 29011}^{\mathchar 29038}_{\mathchar 8235}}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29018_{\mathchar 8511}\mathchar 8235\mathchar 29000^{\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29035\delimiter 84054785}\delimiter 84054785\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349\mathchar 29042\mathchar 12349\mathrm{\mathchar 29042\mathchar 29025\mathchar 29038\mathchar 29035}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28677_{\mathbb{\mathchar 29011}^{\mathchar 29038}_{\mathchar 8235}}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29018_{\mathchar 8511}\delimiter 84054785\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 314

The dual part follows in a symmetric manner:

\mathrm{\mathchar 29042\mathchar 29025\mathchar 29038\mathchar 29035}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29011^{\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29035\delimiter 84054785}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349\mathrm{\mathchar 29042\mathchar 29025\mathchar 29038\mathchar 29035}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28677_{\mathbb{\mathchar 29011}^{\mathchar 29038}_{\mathchar 8235}}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 8704\mathchar 29018_{\mathchar 8511}\mathchar 8704\mathchar 29000^{\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29035\delimiter 84054785}\delimiter 84054785\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349\mathchar 29038\mathchar 8704\mathchar 29042\mathchar 12349\mathrm{\mathchar 29042\mathchar 29025\mathchar 29038\mathchar 29035}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28677_{\mathbb{\mathchar 29011}^{\mathchar 29038}_{\mathchar 8235}}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 8704\mathchar 29018_{\mathchar 8511}\delimiter 84054785\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 314

Second, since
\delimiter 69645069\mathchar 29000^{\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29035\delimiter 84054785}\delimiter 86422285_{\mathchar 28722}\mathchar 12833\mathchar 28720
as
\mathchar 29035\mathchar 12833\mathchar 561
, there exists
\bar{\mathchar 29035}_{\mathrm{\mathchar 29001\mathchar 28996}}\mathchar 12850\mathbb{\mathchar 29006}
such that
\delimiter 69645069\mathchar 29000^{\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29035\delimiter 84054785}\delimiter 86422285_{\mathchar 28722}\mathchar 12604\min\{\mathchar 28949_{\mathchar 29042}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28949_{\mathchar 29042\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721}\}
. This concludes the proof.
∎

On the relation between rank identification and linear convergence.

Considering both rank identification and local linear convergence, it is natural to investigate the relationship of these two phenomena: which one occurs first? Unlike the case of PDHG for LP, it remains unclear whether rank identification is the trigger for linear convergence.

Here, we provide a simple example to discuss the sutble role of rank identification in our proof procedure.

Recall from our analysis (specifically
Lemma

6
) that the R-linear convergence of
\delimiter 69645069\mathchar 29000^{\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29035\delimiter 84054785}\delimiter 86422285_{\mathsf{\mathchar 28998}}
is built upon that of the two sequences

\delimiter 69645069\mathchar 28677_{{\cal\mathchar 29012}_{\mathchar 29011_{\mathchar 8511}}}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29016^{\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29035\delimiter 84054785}\delimiter 84054785\delimiter 86422285_{\mathsf{\mathchar 28998}}\mathchar 12349{\cal\mathchar 29007}\delimiter 67273472\delimiter 69645069\mathchar 29000_{\mathchar 29007}^{\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29035\delimiter 84054785}\delimiter 86422285_{\mathsf{\mathchar 28998}}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 24891\qquad\delimiter 69645069\mathchar 28677_{{\cal\mathchar 29012}_{\mathchar 29016_{\mathchar 8511}}}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29011^{\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29035\delimiter 84054785}\delimiter 84054785\delimiter 86422285_{\mathsf{\mathchar 28998}}\mathchar 12349{\cal\mathchar 29007}\delimiter 67273472\delimiter 69645069\mathchar 29000_{\mathchar 29007}^{\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29035\delimiter 84054785}\delimiter 86422285_{\mathsf{\mathchar 28998}}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 314

(54)

In view of this, we build an SDP instance in which rank identification does not occur and (
54
) fails to hold. So, in the worst case, (
54
) needs rank identification.
We conjecture that, if (
54
) were also necessary for linear convergence (which we are currently unable to prove), then rank identification would occur no later than the onset of the final (R-)linear convergence regime.

Open problems
: In what type of SDPs is rank identification a necessary condition for (R-)linear convergence? Under which conditions will rank identification and (R-)linear convergence occur simultaneously?

10
Discussion: Connections to Metric Subregularity and Local Error Bounds

Our proof framework is closely related to several notions of metric subregularity and local error bounds studied in the literature
[
68
,
41
,
37
,
14
,
24
,
1
]
. Recall that a set-valued mapping
\mathchar 28958\mathchar 12346{\cal\mathchar 29016}\rightrightarrows{\cal\mathchar 29015}
is metric subregular at
\bar{\mathchar 29048}\mathchar 12850{\cal\mathchar 29016}
for
\bar{\mathchar 29047}\mathchar 12850{\cal\mathchar 29015}
with modulus
\mathchar 28948
if
\delimiter 67273472\bar{\mathchar 29048}\mathchar 24891\bar{\mathchar 29047}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12850\mathrm{\mathchar 29031\mathchar 29040\mathchar 29032}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28958\delimiter 84054785
and
\mathchar 569\mathchar 28943\mathchar 12606\mathchar 28720
, s.t.

\displaystyle\mathrm{\mathchar 29028\mathchar 29033\mathchar 29043\mathchar 29044}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29048\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28958^{\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28721}\delimiter 67273472\bar{\mathchar 29047}\delimiter 84054785\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12820\mathchar 28948\mathrm{\mathchar 29028\mathchar 29033\mathchar 29043\mathchar 29044}\delimiter 67273472\bar{\mathchar 29047}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28958\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29048\delimiter 84054785\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 24891\quad\mathchar 568\mathchar 29048\mathchar 12850\mathbb{\mathchar 28994}_{\mathchar 28943}\delimiter 67273472\bar{\mathchar 29048}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 24891

where
\mathbb{\mathchar 28994}_{\mathchar 28943}\delimiter 67273472\bar{\mathchar 29048}\delimiter 84054785
denotes the ball of radius
\mathchar 28943
centered at
\bar{\mathchar 29048}
.
For first-order splitting methods,
\mathchar 28958
is often taken to be a (variant of the) KKT solution mapping defined in (
3
). Let
{\cal\mathchar 29003}
denote the set of KKT points. Then
\bar{\mathchar 29048}
is typically a KKT point
\delimiter 67273472\bar{\mathchar 29016}\mathchar 24891\bar{\mathchar 29049}\mathchar 24891\bar{\mathchar 29011}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12850{\cal\mathchar 29003}
, with
\bar{\mathchar 29047}\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28720
. In this case,
\mathrm{\mathchar 29028\mathchar 29033\mathchar 29043\mathchar 29044}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29048\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28958^{\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28721}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28720\delimiter 84054785\delimiter 84054785
is the distance from
\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29016\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29049\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29011\delimiter 84054785
to
{\cal\mathchar 29003}
(the forward error
[
55
]
), while
\mathrm{\mathchar 29028\mathchar 29033\mathchar 29043\mathchar 29044}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28720\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28958\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29048\delimiter 84054785\delimiter 84054785
corresponds to a (variant of the) KKT residual (the backward error
[
55
]
). Thus, metric subregularity at a KKT point for
\mathchar 28720
naturally yields a local error bound (EB).

Variants of local error bounds.

[
41
]
introduces four types of error bounds, which we now specialize to the ADMM-for-SDP setting.
The first three are equivalent
[
41
, Appendix A]
: full error bound (FEB)
[
41
, Eq. (1.7)]
, Proximal EB-I
[
41
, Eq. (1.12)]
, and Proximal EB-II
[
41
, Eq. (1.14)]
. We only state Proximal EB-I, which also coincides with the metric subregularity of the KKT mapping
{\cal\mathchar 29010}
in
[
24
, Eq. (7)]
. It is derived from the projection form of the KKT mapping:

\displaystyle\mathrm{\mathchar 29028\mathchar 29033\mathchar 29043\mathchar 29044}\delimiter 67273472\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29016\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29049\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29011\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 24891{\cal\mathchar 29003}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12820\mathchar 28948\delimiter 67273472\delimiter 69645069{\cal\mathchar 28993}\mathchar 29016\mathchar 8704\mathchar 29026\delimiter 86422285_{\mathchar 28722}\mathchar 8235\delimiter 69645069{\cal\mathchar 28993}^{\mathchar 8707}\mathchar 29049\mathchar 8235\mathchar 29011\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28995\delimiter 86422285_{\mathsf{\mathchar 28998}}\mathchar 8235\delimiter 69645069\mathchar 29011\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28677_{\mathbb{\mathchar 29011}^{\mathchar 29038}_{\mathchar 8235}}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29011\mathchar 8704\mathchar 29016\delimiter 84054785\delimiter 86422285_{\mathsf{\mathchar 28998}}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 24891

(55)

\displaystyle\mathchar 568\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29016\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29049\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29011\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12850\mathbb{\mathchar 28994}_{\mathchar 28943}\delimiter 67273472\delimiter 67273472\bar{\mathchar 29016}\mathchar 24891\bar{\mathchar 29049}\mathchar 24891\bar{\mathchar 29011}\delimiter 84054785\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 314

The fourth is the partial error bound (PEB)
[

, Definition 5.2]
:

\displaystyle\mathrm{\mathchar 29028\mathchar 29033\mathchar 29043\mathchar 29044}\delimiter 67273472\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29016\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29049\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29011\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 24891{\cal\mathchar 29003}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12820\mathchar 28948\delimiter 67273472\delimiter 69645069{\cal\mathchar 28993}\mathchar 29016\mathchar 8704\mathchar 29026\delimiter 86422285_{\mathchar 28722}\mathchar 8235\delimiter 69645069{\cal\mathchar 28993}^{\mathchar 8707}\mathchar 29049\mathchar 8235\mathchar 29011\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28995\delimiter 86422285_{\mathsf{\mathchar 28998}}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 24891

\displaystyle\mathchar 568\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29016\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29049\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29011\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12850\mathbb{\mathchar 28994}_{\mathchar 28943}\delimiter 67273472\delimiter 67273472\bar{\mathchar 29016}\mathchar 24891\bar{\mathchar 29049}\mathchar 24891\bar{\mathchar 29011}\delimiter 84054785\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 24891\ \mathchar 29016\mathchar 12823\mathchar 28720\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29011\mathchar 12823\mathchar 28720\mathchar 24891\left\delimiter 0\mathchar 29016\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29011\right\delimiter 0\mathchar 314

Note that the term
\delimiter 69645069\mathchar 29011\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28677_{\mathbb{\mathchar 29011}^{\mathchar 29038}_{\mathchar 8235}}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29011\mathchar 8704\mathchar 29016\delimiter 84054785\delimiter 86422285_{\mathsf{\mathchar 28998}}
in (

) is always
\mathchar 28720
whenever
\mathchar 29016\mathchar 12823\mathchar 28720\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29011\mathchar 12823\mathchar 28720\mathchar 24891\left\delimiter 0\mathchar 29016\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29011\right\delimiter 0

\displaystyle\mathrm{\mathchar 29028\mathchar 29033\mathchar 29043\mathchar 29044}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29018\mathchar 24891{\cal\mathchar 29018}_{\mathchar 8511}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12820\mathchar 28948\delimiter 69645069\underbrace{\mathchar 8704{\cal\mathchar 29008}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28677_{\mathbb{\mathchar 29011}^{\mathchar 29038}_{\mathchar 8235}}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29018\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 8704\widetilde{\mathchar 29016}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 8235{\cal\mathchar 29008}^{\mathchar 12863}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28677_{\mathbb{\mathchar 29011}^{\mathchar 29038}_{\mathchar 8235}}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 8704\mathchar 29018\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28955\mathchar 28995\delimiter 84054785}_{\mathchar 12349\mathchar 12346\mathchar 28942\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29018\delimiter 84054785}\delimiter 86422285_{\mathsf{\mathchar 28998}}\mathchar 24891\ \mathchar 568\mathchar 29018\mathchar 12850\mathbb{\mathchar 28994}_{\mathchar 28943}\delimiter 67273472\bar{\mathchar 29018}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 24891

(57)

where
\bar{\mathchar 29018}\mathchar 12850{\cal\mathchar 29018}_{\mathchar 8511}
.

Proposition 4

(PEB
\mathchar 12841
DRS-EB)
.

In the ADMM for SDP setting, PEB (
56
) implies DRS-EB (
57
) (up to a different modulus).

Proof.

Fix
\bar{\mathchar 29018}\mathchar 12850{\cal\mathchar 29018}_{\mathchar 8511}
and
\mathchar 29018\mathchar 12850\mathbb{\mathchar 28994}_{\mathchar 28943}\delimiter 67273472\bar{\mathchar 29018}\delimiter 84054785

\displaystyle\mathchar 29016\mathchar 12346\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28677_{\mathbb{\mathchar 29011}^{\mathchar 29038}_{\mathchar 8235}}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29018\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 24891\ \mathchar 29011\mathchar 12346\mathchar 12349\mathchar 8704{{\mathchar 28721\over\mathchar 28955}}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28677_{\mathbb{\mathchar 29011}^{\mathchar 29038}_{\mathchar 8235}}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 8704\mathchar 29018\delimiter 84054785\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 24891\ \mathchar 29049\mathchar 12346\mathchar 12349\delimiter 67273472{\cal\mathchar 28993}{\cal\mathchar 28993}^{\mathchar 8707}\delimiter 84054785^{\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28721}{\cal\mathchar 28993}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28995\mathchar 8704\mathchar 29011\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 24891

\displaystyle\bar{\mathchar 29016}\mathchar 12346\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28677_{\mathbb{\mathchar 29011}^{\mathchar 29038}_{\mathchar 8235}}\delimiter 67273472\bar{\mathchar 29018}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 24891\ \bar{\mathchar 29011}\mathchar 12346\mathchar 12349\mathchar 8704{{\mathchar 28721\over\mathchar 28955}}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28677_{\mathbb{\mathchar 29011}^{\mathchar 29038}_{\mathchar 8235}}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 8704\bar{\mathchar 29018}\delimiter 84054785\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 24891\ \bar{\mathchar 29049}\mathchar 12346\mathchar 12349\delimiter 67273472{\cal\mathchar 28993}{\cal\mathchar 28993}^{\mathchar 8707}\delimiter 84054785^{\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28721}{\cal\mathchar 28993}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28995\mathchar 8704\bar{\mathchar 29011}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 314

Thus,
\delimiter 67273472\bar{\mathchar 29016}\mathchar 24891\bar{\mathchar 29049}\mathchar 24891\bar{\mathchar 29011}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12850{\cal\mathchar 29003}
and
\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29016\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29049\mathchar 24891\mathchar 29011\delimiter 84054785
satisfies conditions in (

) (up to a different ball radius). Moreover,

The proof of Theorem
5
again highlights the importance of Theorem
2
: without it, neither (
58
) nor (
61

Proximal EB-I
Proximal EB-II

Connections to hidden polyhedrality.
In the literature
[
37
,
68
]
, various forms of hidden polyhedrality have been exploited as special cases of metric subregularity and local error bounds. Specifically,
[
37
]
defines local polyhedrality under the partly smoothness setting. Their framework further requires strict complementarity at the KKT point, together with vanishing higher-order terms
[
37
, Proposition 5.2]
,
i.e.,

\mathchar 28681^{\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29035\delimiter 84054785}\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28720
in (
18
). On the other hand,
[
68
, Assumption 1.3]
introduces structured polyhedricity, a model-structural variational assumption. This assumption requires one of ADMM’s block functions to be piecewise linear-quadratic and the other to be locally strongly convex. Although these conditions have successfully established local linear convergence in piecewise linear-quadratic optimization settings, they do not apply directly to ADMM for SDP due to the non-polyhedrality of
\mathchar 28942_{\mathbb{\mathchar 29011}^{\mathchar 29038}_{\mathchar 8235}}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 8705\delimiter 84054785
.

It is natural to ask whether further connections hold between the other error bounds and the SC condition:

Open problems
: In ADMM for SDP, does the SC condition (Assumption
2
) imply PEB (
56
)? Does the SC condition (Assumption
2
) imply Proximal EB-I (
55
) (and hence FEB and Proximal EB-II)?

11
Conclusion

We established a new sufficient condition for the local linear convergence of the Alternating Direction Method of Multipliers (ADMM) in solving semidefinite programming (SDP) problems. Contrary to the conventional belief that ADMM is inherently slow for SDPs, we demonstrated that when the converged primal–dual optimal solutions satisfy strict complementarity, ADMM exhibits local linear convergence, regardless of nondegeneracy conditions. Our theoretical analysis is grounded in a direct local linearization of the ADMM operator and a refined error bound for the projection onto the positive semidefinite cone, revealing the anisotropic nature of projection residuals and improving previous bounds.

Extensive numerical experiments validated our theoretical findings, showing that ADMM achieves local linear convergence across a variety of SDP instances, including those where nondegeneracy fails. Furthermore, we identified cases where ADMM struggles to reach high accuracy, linking these difficulties to near violations of strict complementarity. This observation aligns with recent results in linear programming.

Our numerical results also revealed intriguing connections between rank identification and linear convergence. While we provided a qualitative analysis, a complete understanding remains open. Future work could further investigate this relationship, examine whether linear convergence can occur in the absence of both nondegeneracy and strict complementarity, and develop new algorithms to accelerate ADMM and other first-order methods.

, Proposition 3.4]

.

Let

Appendix B
Local Linear Convergence with Nondegeneracy but without SC

\displaystyle\mathchar 12821\max\{\delimiter 69645069\mathchar 29018_{\mathchar 29016\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28720}\delimiter 86422285_{\mathchar 28722}\mathchar 24891\delimiter 69645069\mathchar 29018_{\mathchar 29011\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28720}\delimiter 86422285_{\mathchar 28722}\}\mathchar 24891

\displaystyle\delimiter 69645069\mathchar 29018_{\mathchar 28720}\delimiter 86422285_{\mathchar 28722}

\displaystyle\mathchar 12820\delimiter 69645069\mathchar 29018\delimiter 86422285_{\mathchar 28722}\mathchar 8235{{\mathchar 28721\over\mathchar 28722}}\min\{\mathchar 28949_{\mathchar 29042}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28949_{\mathchar 29042\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721}\}\mathchar 12820\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28949_{\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28949_{\mathchar 29038}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 8235{{\mathchar 28721\over\mathchar 28722}}\min\{\mathchar 28949_{\mathchar 29042}\mathchar 24891\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28949_{\mathchar 29042\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721}\}

and recall
\delimiter 69645069\mathchar 29018_{\mathchar 29016\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28720}\delimiter 86422285_{\mathchar 28722}\mathchar 12821\mathchar 28949_{\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 8704{{\mathchar 28721\over\mathchar 28722}}\mathchar 28949_{\mathchar 29042}
.

. Since

\mathchar 28995_{\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 12349{{\mathchar 28721\over\mathchar 28722}}
, which is trivial.

•
